The authors claim that, given the epidemic did not spread to others in the colony post-arrival, it was likely their condition was exacerbated by starvation and other allied nutritional disorders. 17  Michael Flynn, The Second Fleet: Britain’s Grim Convict Armada of 1790 (Sydney: Library of Australian History, 1993). 18  A court of inquiry was held in Sydney that, among other things, tells of convicts drinking their own urine for want of water. The full inquiry can be found in Historical Records of Australia (HRA), series 1, vol. 2, 1797–1800, 60–8. 19  Hyman, ‘Convicts and Convictions’; Bateson, The Convict Ships, 182. The Hillsborough embarked three hundred male convicts with ninety-five expiring on the journey, thirty-one per cent. 20  Donald R. McNeil, ‘Medical Care aboard Australia-Bound Convict Ships, 1786–1840’, Bulletin of the History of Medicine 26, no. 2 (March–April 1952), 125, doi.org/10.5694/j.1326-5377.1952.tb94662.x. Atlas embarked 151 and sixty-three female convicts. Twenty-eight male and two female convicts died on the voyage, 18.5 per cent and three per cent, respectively. 21  ‘Diseases on the Surry originated in the neglect of the Master and the surgeon’, tabled in a report by William Redfern, assistant surgeon, in the colony and endorsed by Governor Macquarie and sent to Lord Bathurst. HRA, series 1, vol. 8, 274–5, 279–81, 284, 295, cited in McNeil, ‘Medical Care’, 126. The Surry embarked two hundred male convicts and experienced thirty-six convict deaths or eighteen per cent. 22  Emma Christopher, ‘“Ten Thousand Times Worse than the Convicts”: Rebellious Sailors, Convict Transportation and the Struggle for Freedom, 1787–1800’, Journal of Australian Colonial History 5 (2004): 30–39.
Women’s lives in a fragmented archive 
103 
for convict transportation to Australia. What is perhaps more unusual is the poor treatment of the female convicts. Was it his slaving past that inured him to their suffering? Was it that he had brought his mistress on board with him and therefore did not take a convict wife of his own, viewing them purely as cargo? This paper examines the fate of the female convicts on board the Neptune and argues that they suffered particularly due to Trail’s attitudes towards them. 
Cramped life on board transports The convicts and crew began boarding the three transports in September 1789. According to the quartermaster, John Beale, who joined the ship on 13 November 1789, most of the female convicts had already embarked.23 The ships were initially moored in the Thames, taking male prisoners from hulks and female prisoners from Newgate, as well as other women conveyed by wagon from all over Britain. While it is difficult to ascertain exactly how much space there was on board for each person, there is no doubt that conditions were incredibly overcrowded. We know that approximately 420 male convicts and seventy-eight female convicts boarded the Neptune. Further, there were at least forty-seven New South Wales Corps personnel and an additional fifteen or so wives and children of New South Wales Corps and convicts.24 The law stipulated that ships required a crew of six men plus one boy per one hundred tonnes and, as the Neptune was over eight hundred tonnes, we can assume that the crew numbered at least fifty.25 It is thus estimated that the Neptune carried at least six hundred people plus supplies, victuals and live animals. Life on board was indeed crowded. To put this into context, the 1,250 or so people of the First Fleet were dispersed across eleven ships. The Second Fleet carried 1,100 people dispersed across just three ships.26 
The only firsthand account of the Neptune’s journey from a female perspective was the journal of Elizabeth Macarthur, who was not a convict but the wife of an army officer, Lieutenant John Macarthur, who, as part of the New South Wales Corps, was to effectively run the colony. Her account began optimistically, but quickly deteriorated when the family’s self-perceived social standing and notions of hierarchy were not reciprocated by those in charge.27 Trouble began while the Neptune was still moored at Gravesend, when Captain Nicholas Nepean, also of the New South Wales Corps (and a brother of the undersecretary of the Home Office, Evan Nepean), decided to quit the cabin he shared with the Macarthurs and accept a berth in the cuddy, an 
23  TS11/381/1186, 1–12, John Beale deposition. 24  For numbers of NSW Corps, see Historical Records of New South Wales (HRNSW), vol. 1, part 2 (1783–1792): 311. Seven officers and forty-four privates of the New South Wales Corps were on the Neptune as well as six women (wives of NSW Corps) and three children. 25  Accounts and Papers Relating to Convicts on Board the Hulks, and Those Transported to New South Wales (House of Commons, 1792): 310–14. 26  These numbers do not include ships’ crews for either the First or Second Fleets. Numbers are approximate. 27  Volume 10: Elizabeth Macarthur Journal and Correspondence, 1789–1840, State Library of New South Wales (SLNSW), collection.sl.nsw.gov.au/record/9O4oVWpn.
Australian Journal of Biography and History: No. 8, 2024 
104 
additional set of cabins built on top of the upper deck, with the ship’s captain, who at that time was Captain Gilbert. It was decided, probably by both Gilbert and Nepean, that the space in the great cabin should be reallocated, and half set aside for the female convicts, presumably due to overcrowding. A partition was erected separating the Macarthurs from the women convicts. However, living in such proximity was intolerable to the Macarthurs: 
A slight partition was erected, which was thought fully sufficient to separate us from the set of abandoned creatures that were to inhabit the other part, and the only satisfaction or concession that Mr Macarthur could obtain for this cruel encroachment upon our rights was a promise and assurance that a passage, which from our quarter gallery communicated with the upper cabin, should always be open for our use and even for our servants. This assurance, trifling as it may appear, was to us an inestimable advantage, as the division in the cabin had rendered the common passage to the deck totally dark, and added to this, it was always filled with convicts and their constant attendants, filth and vermin. The altercations and little disputes that the concluding of this business occasioned created a coldness between Captain Nepean, the master of this ship (Captain Gilbert), and Mr Macarthur, and at last terminated in a cessation of every kind of intercourse, except on duty with the one, and on business with the other.28 
Despite the fact that the Macarthurs had a small balcony on which they could take in the sea air, John was overheard complaining that it: 
was impossible to live in his cabin owing to the stench of the bucketts [sic] belonging to the convict women of a morning, and [he] hoped that the captain would think on some mode of remedy.29 
This sense of entitlement was grating for Gilbert, already irritated with the Macarthurs for appealing to the first mate about the matter rather than taking it directly to him, which he considered a breach of hierarchy; a serious matter in maritime life. The Macarthurs continued to complain about their situation to Gilbert, who showed little, if any, sympathy, resulting in John challenging him to a bloodless duel. Gilbert had previously held the same rank in the navy—lieutenant—as Macarthur held in the army. However, he was now the captain of the ship and Macarthur’s insolence would have been seen as a serious breach of both code and honour. The duel was scheduled for 4 pm on 28 September at the Fountain Tavern on Plymouth Dock.30 Each man appointed a second, stood back-to-back and paced out their length, then turned and fired, reloaded and fired again. Neither man was injured, and reputations were restored when each man agreed to live in harmony, as both were satisfied with the 
28  Sibella Macarthur Onslow, ed., Some Early Records of the Macarthurs of Camden (Sydney: Angus & Robertson, 1914). 29  HRNSW, vol. 2, 428, Surgeon Harris’s account of the quarrel between Captain Gilbert and Lieutenant Macarthur. 30  HRNSW, vol. 2, 428.
Women’s lives in a fragmented archive 
105 
other’s apologies. Somewhat oddly, when Nepean came back on board the next day, having spent the night ashore, he decided, for some unknown reason, to take charge of the convicts, ordering the first mate, Anstis, Gilbert’s second-in-command, to hand over the keys to the male and female convicts’ rooms. Gilbert only learned of this when he returned to the ship later that evening. Once again, this action of assuming control was a direct challenge to the captain’s authority, not to mention a complete contravention of the orders given to the captain by Camden, Calvert & King. 
It is worth pondering the specific lore and laws of the sea. When people boarded a ship in the late eighteenth century, they entered into an entirely different set of social systems. Shipping and maritime life were central to the times and integral to global expansion. The industry had enabled exploration, colonisation and migration, and trade had opened up across the world, including with India, China, America and the West Indies, as European countries competed for resources. Maritime trade saw the East India Company prosper into the largest company in the world at its peak. The many months, sometimes years, that crews spent at sea necessitated strict hierarchical and onboard social systems to maintain a functional outfit capable of living in close confines, on salted rations and working in shifts with barely any time off. Captains were at constant risk of desertions or mutiny so that their authority was paramount and never to be questioned. Richard Henry Dana’s account of his two years on board a merchant vessel remains one of the most vivid descriptions of life and lore at sea during this period. It details the strict regularity of maritime life and of the unquestioned hierarchy on board: 
The captain is lord paramount … accountable to no one and must be obeyed in everything, without question. He has the power to turn his officers off duty, and even to break them.31 
The insult occasioned by both Macarthur’s complaints and his challenge to a duel, and Nepean’s assumption of control over the convicts, cannot be overstated. The day after the duel, a riot broke out among the female convicts. Surgeon Harris was summoned to the women’s quarters where he found two New South Wales Corps sentinels holding back ‘a girl’ who was swearing ‘vehemently’ that she would kill ‘two or three women at the other end of the room’.32 Harris ordered that she be let go, not thinking she would follow through on her threats, but as soon as she was released, she flew at the other women. Harris then decided that she should be put in irons. Soon after the girl had been restrained, Gilbert discovered what had happened and shouted 
31  Richard Henry Dana, Two Years before the Mast: A Personal Narrative of Life at Sea (Harper and Brothers, 1840), ch. 3. 32  HRNSW, vol. 2, 428. With very little to go on but that Harris called her ‘a girl’ and the others he called ‘women’, it’s not possible to pinpoint who of the seventy-eight women on board it might be. There were two ‘girls’ of fifteen years of age, Anne Barker and Mary Cooksley. Mary Cooksey died of a snakebite in 1812 in Liverpool, aged thirty-seven. She was married twice and had two children. See Sydney Gazette, 21 November 1812. Ann Barker married Second Fleet convict John Boxley of the Scarborough. They had two children together and she died in 1804 aged twenty-nine. Needham, The Women of the 1790 Neptune.
Australian Journal of Biography and History: No. 8, 2024 
106 
down the hatchway at Harris asking what he thought he was doing by deciding discipline on his ship. Gilbert came into the women’s room where a scuffle broke out between him, Harris and a sentinel of the New South Wales Corps guarding the women, the sentinel telling Gilbert that he would run him through with his bayonet if he did not desist (from fighting Harris).33 Gilbert rallied the women, saying they were being ill-treated by the corps and stormed out of their cell and back to his own cabin where he began drinking and ordered some muskets be loaded and brought to his cabin. Later that evening when Nepean, who commanded the corps, returned to the ship and found what had happened, he too ordered that a store of arms be loaded and given to the corps. All this without having even left England! 
While the causes and consequences of the riot among the women were unknown, we do know that conditions in the quarters were crowded. We also know that convicts were hungry, because almost all of the crew gave evidence about convicts bartering their clothing for food before the fleet left English shores. To give three examples: 
One [convict] sold a pair of shoes for 3 biscuits.34 
Beale bought a pair of shoes for 4 biscuits.35 
According to Churchill, ‘the convicts were in such distress that he bought a pair of trousers for half a cupful of smallbread, a hat for two biscuits and a few pease and new shoes for 6 pence and little bread’.36 
The convicts were assigned a mess of six people when they boarded, probably the same six people with whom they shared a cabin. One of the six would have been tasked with fetching and distributing the food and water. Each mess was allocated a pudding bag, which was filled with food, dropped into the boiling water of the copper, and retrieved again when cooked through. The ration of provisions for each mess of six female convicts for seven days included: 
Bread lb 20 
Flour lb 12 
Beef lb 7 
Pork lb 6 
Pease pints 12 
Butter 1 ½ lb 
Rice lb 2 
Tea lb ¼ 
Sugar lb 3. 
33  HRNSW, vol. 2, 428, Surgeon Harris’s account. 34  TS11/381/1186, 29–32, depositions of John Roger and Robert Fletcher, harriers. 35  TS11/381/1186, 1–12, Beale deposition. 36  TS11/381/1186, 12–18, Churchill deposition.
Women’s lives in a fragmented archive 
107 
For the male convicts per mess rations were: 
Bread lb 16 
Flour lb 12 
Beef lb 14 
Pork lb 8 
Pease pints 12 
Butter lb 1 1/2 
Rice lb 2.37 
Quartermaster Beale testified that there was a hierarchy among convicts: 
the convicts appointed to distribute to the provisions and the water frequently kept back from the sick and weak, [those] in good health ate and drank the principal part of the allowance, and there was no control of the dispossession thereof. The strongest man who got hold of the keg first and drank as much as he liked and then the next strongest got hold of the keg and drank in like manner and so progressively until the same was emptied by which means the sick and weak either had very little or none at all.38 
Before the fleet left England, it appears that all passengers on board the Neptune engaged in power plays of various sorts: the riot among the convict women, the purloining of the victuals among the convict men, and the order of hierarchy between the officers of the ship and the officers of the New South Wales Corps. The conflict resulted in Gilbert being stood down and Donald Trail, captain of the Surprize, being ordered to take over, while the first mate of the Neptune, Anstis, was assigned to take over the Surprize. Although Nepean succeeded in having Gilbert replaced, he was also humiliated, as the issue of whose power the convicts came under was clarified and any doubt was dispelled. The convicts were the responsibility of the ships’ captains until landed in the colony. The corps was there to act as guards—and no more. The conflict shows that a new set of social systems had been entered into, one distinctly maritime in nature, and that showed some considerable contempt for those who did not know the rules or assume their rightful position on board.39 
The contract with Camden, Calvert & King There is an argument that the contractual arrangement the Home Office had made with the private company Camden, Calvert & King to transport the convicts to New South Wales was a major factor in their maltreatment. A former slave trading firm, Camden, Calvert & King charged half that of the contractor for the First 
37  HRNSW, vol. 1, part 2, 65. 38  TS11/381/1186, 1–12, Beale deposition. 39  HRNSW, vol. 1, part 2, 279.
Australian Journal of Biography and History: No. 8, 2024 
108 
Fleet. Indeed, the difference between the sum paid to First Fleet contractor Richard Williams (£54,000) compared with that paid to Camden, Calvert & King (£22,370) bears scrutiny.40 
The Second Fleet’s far more marginal contract resulted in skimping on the provisioning and outfitting of the transports, negatively impacting the convicts’ wellbeing. The poor provisioning was a consistent complaint in the depositions of the twenty-three crew, many of whom testified that their food allowance was deficient and that the convicts were literally starving. Joseph Collins described the beef as ‘perfectly carrion’ and complained that he had ‘seen better meat given to the hounds in England. I was well satisfied that this meat would give them (the convicts) the flux, which was the case.’41 Trail was instructed to be as economical as possible with the convicts’ rations and to take every opportunity to maximise profits.42 The depositions show that conditions on board were dire and there were shortages of food and water. There are numerous accounts of convicts taking food out of the mouths of other convicts who had died mid-mouthful. There are also reports of convicts stealing food from the shipboard hogs; of a convict taking an oatmeal poultice off the wound on another convict’s leg to eat; and of convicts keeping quiet about deceased fellow prisoners, even when shackled together, so that they could receive extra rations.43 Camden, Calvert & King were well-known slave traders, having been responsible for some seventy-seven recorded slave ventures between 1766 and 1807. Trail was involved in at least four of those slaving ventures.44 
The skimping extended to the preparation of the ship, with the Neptune widely described by the crew as ill-equipped for so long a journey. Camden, Calvert & King bought the ship in 1789, by which time she was already in poor condition, having undertaken four lengthy sea voyages: two to India and two to China.45 According to Edward Hoare, carpenter on the ship, she had cost £1,600 with all her rigging and stores.46 To give some context to its state, a new ship at the time cost £30,000 
40  HRNSW, vol. 1, part 2, 279. 41  TS11/381/1186, 50–3, Joseph Collins deposition. 42  HRNSW, vol. 2, 751: 
all the provisions put on board … for the convicts and troops unexpended, together with the watercasks and stores, [you are] to dispose of them … to the best advantage for our account. 
43  Daniel Conolly, deposition mariner (TS11/381/1186, 42) described a convict by the name of James Tucker who kept the body of one of the convicts below for several days until he stunk very much in order to get the allowance. Conolly also said he saw Tucker ‘take the poultice made of oatmeal off the sore on a man’s leg and eat it’. James Tucker survived the journey, later becoming the hangman on Norfolk Island—not a job that anyone wanted and a job that often attracted some inducement, such as not being charged for a crime or limiting their sentence. 44  David Eltis, Stephen D. Behrendt, David Richardson and Herbert S. Klein, The Trans-Atlantic Slave Trade: A Database on CD-ROM (Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 1999). See also Ken Cozens, ‘Captain Donald Trail: Hero or Villain?’, Merchant Networks, accessed 11 August 2023, www.danbyrnes.com.au/networks/periods/ 1775 after/1789calvert.htm. 45  Gary Sturgess, Neptune the Biography, unpublished manuscript. 46  TS11/381/1186, 23, Edward Hoare deposition.
Women’s lives in a fragmented archive 
109 
and a complete refurbishment, £10,000.47 The Neptune’s condition would become a major issue in the way that it was managed on its voyage to Botany Bay. Every one of the mariners on board complained about the rigging, the rottenness of the sails and the lack of caulking. Any experienced sailor could see that the ship was in no fit state for a journey to the other side of the world. According to Beale: 
the Gunners Mate, Quarter Master, Botswain’s Mate and several seamen quitted the ship at Gravesend because of … the way the ship was equipped. [On their passage to Plymouth] the maintop Gallant broke twice in very little wind.48 
It is claimed up to two hundred crew deserted the ship before it had left England, and many more tried.49 
Finally, because the contractors were on such a tight budget, Camden, Calvert & King instructed the masters of the ships to make haste and refrain from stopping unless absolutely necessary. Consequently, whereas the First Fleet and the Lady Juliana made three stops on the journey to New South Wales, the Second Fleet made just one stop. Stopping allowed for fresh provisions, water, meat and vegetables, and thus helped to stave off scurvy. However, as mentioned, it is clear from the instructions given to Trail that he was to take every opportunity to increase profits. His orders stated: 
proceed with all convenient speed … Should it be found necessary on your passage to New South Wales to touch at any place to take refreshments and water we particularly recommend the Cape of Good Hope being most immediately in your way and where the least time will be lost. You are, at Port Jackson or China (at your option), to receive from the Scarborough all the provisions put on board her for the convicts and troops unexpended, together with the watercasks and stores, and to dispose of them, with your own and those on board the Surprize to the best advantage for our account.50 
Trail takes over the Neptune and instils a culture of fear After the fracas between the New South Wales Corps and the ship’s officers, all the ships of the Second Fleet converged on Stokes Bay, Portsmouth, on 13 December, the last port of call before departure for Botany Bay. Several people joined the Neptune there who would significantly alter convicts’ onboard experience. They included Trail, William Elrington and the ship’s surgeon, William Gray. Also joining was the naval agent, John Shapcote, whose job was to ensure that the masters of the ships fulfilled their contractual agreements regarding the delivery of convicts. A single 
47  C. Northcote Parkinson, Trade in the Eastern Seas, 1793–1813 (New York: Frank Cass & Co., 1966), 134, 146. With thanks to Gary L. Sturgess for this reference. 48  TS11/381/1186, 1–12, Beale deposition. 49  Two hundred crew deserted the ship before she left England. Prosecutor Mr Thomas Evans to Under Secretary King, 20 January 1792, HRNSW, vol. 2, 462. 50  HRNSW, vol. 2, 751.
Australian Journal of Biography and History: No. 8, 2024 
110 
female convict, thirty-year-old Mary McDonaugh, the final female convict to board the Second Fleet, was boarded at this last stop. One more passenger was to be taken on board, the only fee-paying passenger of the Second Fleet, D’Arcy Wentworth. In addition, there were a number of new crew members recruited to make up for those who had deserted. When Trail took over the Neptune, it was not just a ship whose officers were vying for power but a ship whose crew were deserting in large numbers. 
Trail used ‘crimps’ to recruit more crew, an oft-used method in the late eighteenth century to attract crew to ships. They acted like scouts, frequenting taverns around the docks and other places sailors might be found, spruiking a ship in a bid to attract crew. In the case of the Neptune, prospective crew were told that they could expect an Indiaman’s allowance of provisions and that each man would be entitled to take an onboard wife for the duration of the journey.51 According to the mariner Joseph Collins, ‘there was a parcel of fine women whom the Captain intended should be let among the crew as soon as the ship was got clear out to sea’.52 John Rogers and Robert Fletcher, mariners, also made depositions about promises made by Trail that once the ship was at sea the women should be permitted to go among the ships’ company.53 Daniel Conolly of the HMS Pearl (recently returned from the Mediterranean) was induced to leave his ship bound for Virginia because he: 
was told there was a parcel of fine women on board with their hats and their feathers flying and promised by the Captain that the women would be all amongst them. 
Such was the lure of women on board that Conolly, who was already signed up on His Majesty’s ship, deserted in favour of the Neptune. Having just returned home from a journey of two years at sea he had no intention of going back out so soon but for the lure of an onboard wife.54 
Contrary to what was promised, once the ship was out to sea and the crew had no opportunity of deserting, Trail’s tune changed. He did not allow the women among the crew. In fact, the captain positively forbade any interaction and beat anyone who dared approach or speak to the women. The first crewman to try was John Scott. According to Quartermaster Beale: 
Soon after the ship took her departure, John Scott, ‘a very able and good seaman’ was directed by the Chief Mate (Elrington) to work with some other men on the poop and whilst he was so working some of the women went on the poop to take in some cloathes [sic] which had been drying whereupon the said Donald Trail seeing Scott talking to the women went down and asked him what he did there … before he had time to speak, Trail knocked him 
51  TS11/381/1186, 12–18, Churchill deposition. 52  TS11/381/1186, 50–3, Collins deposition. 53  TS11/381/1186, 29–32, Fletcher and Rogers depositions. 54  TS11/381/1186, 41, Conolly deposition.
Women’s lives in a fragmented archive 
111 
down and beat and kicked him most unmercifully and when he got up Trail collared and beat him again and ordered him to be put in irons on the Poop with positive orders that he should have no other food than bread and water which was accordingly done and Scott was in irons from 6pm that evening till 11 am the next morning when Scott was ordered to be tied to the ‘drain shrouder’ to be flogged but after he was tied up and his shirt removed he was by the interference of Captain Nepean released but Donald Trail said it was in his power to hang him for making free with the King’s property, meaning the women convicts.55 
Beale went on to say that crew members were frequently beaten for speaking with the women, but: 
not everyone was treated the same. The captain himself … brought on board with him a companion and passed her off as his wife. First mate William Elrington, Robert Gardiner the second mate and William Gray the surgeon as well as John Shapcote the agent selected to themselves companions from among the female convicts with whom they cohabited as man with their wives.56 
Catherine Crowley Crowley was tried at Stafford on 30 July 1788 for stealing a chest of clothing and  some sheets.57 She would have been sixteen years old at the time of her trial and eighteen at the time of transportation. She gave birth to her first son, William, on the journey to Norfolk Island in 1790; he took his father’s surname, Wentworth. Later, she bore D’Arcy Wentworth another son, D’Arcy. The family returned to Sydney in 1796. Crowley died near Parramatta in 1800, aged twentyeight. Her sons would go on to be educated in England and her eldest son would become a prominent colonist as one of the first Europeans to find a way through the Blue Mountains. He helped to draft the New South Wales Constitution, was an author, barrister and stateman.58 Crowley’s second son Major D’Arcy Wentworth died in Tasmania in 1861. A curious thing about Crowley and Wentworth’s relationship, which began on the Second Fleet, is that it is never mentioned—not by Elizabeth Macarthur in her diaries, despite the fact that he was a ‘gentleman’, and not by the crew, who mention by name others who formed relationships with convict women. 
55  TS11/381/1186, 1–12, Beale, deposition. 56  TS11/381/1186, 1–12, Beale deposition. 57  Anne Needham, The Women Transported on the 1790 Neptune (Dural: A. Needham, 1988). 58  J. J. Auchmuty, ‘Wentworth, D’Arcy (1762–1827)’, Australian Dictionary of Biography, National Centre of Biography, The Australian National University, published first in hardcopy 1967, accessed online 11 August 2023, adb.anu.edu.au/biography/wentworth-darcy-1545/text3917.
Australian Journal of Biography and History: No. 8, 2024 
112 
Similarly, Darcy Wentworth cohabited with Catherine Crowley, a convict, and although he is not mentioned in the depositions, we know about the relationship because Crowley gave birth to his child in early August aboard the Surprize while two hundred convicts were being transported to Norfolk Island from Sydney. Thus, while Trail forbade regular crew the ability to form connections with the female convicts, despite promises to the contrary, their seniors were not subject to the same restrictions. It was almost considered a right to be given a woman for the journey.59 
Women Unlike the men, the women of the Neptune were not ironed and had the range of the poop deck to exercise and take in some air. Despite this, some twenty-five per cent of women aboard the Neptune died. Had they been given the opportunity of fraternising with the crew, of performing tasks for food or payment (such as sewing for the crew), I argue that this would have improved their prospects of survival. Christopher has argued that women did have agency as to whether to submit to an onboard sexual partnership. Other historians, such as Portia Robinson, talk of forced submission, perhaps not violent, no aggravated assault, but submission by necessity, and by the confronting alternative of starvation and cruel treatment.60 There were advantages to such unions, including better and more food, better lodgings in less crowded conditions and better treatment.61 This was certainly the case on the Lady Juliana, which consisted entirely of female convicts, and arrived two weeks ahead of the Neptune and experienced just five convict deaths out of 220. When the Lady Juliana arrived at Sydney, according to the historian Sian Rees, the passengers were healthier than when they left England. It was widely reported that every crewman on board, some sixty men, took an ‘onboard wife’ for the duration of the journey.62 As one Lady Juliana convict wrote: ‘We arrived here safe after a long voyage, in very good health … what a difference between us and them [of the Neptune] … God bless our good Agent.’63 
59  There is further evidence from throughout the transportation period that women convicts were forced to submit to sex. In 1819, a resident of Sydney, speaking of his two domestic servants, who were both sexually abused by the crew and the King’s men, declared: 
One of them told me that when in bed she received an order from the captain to come on deck, which order she was obliged to obey, when she was put into the boat with other [women: ‘the youngest and handsomest’] and sent off to the Kings’ ships. This was not the only time they were sent off during the passage. 
See Charles White, Early Australian History: Convict Life in New South Wales and Van Diemen’s Land. Parts 1 & 2 (Bathurst: C. & G. S. White ‘Free Press Office’, 1889), ch. VII. The Bigge Report of 1822 details similar occurrences. 60  Portia Robinson, The Women of Botany Bay: A Reinterpretation of the Role of Women in the Origins of Australian Society (Ringwood: Penguin, 1993). 61  Christopher, ‘The Slave Trade’. 62  Sian Rees, The Floating Brothel: The Extraordinary True Story of an Eighteenth-Century Ship and Its Cargo of Female Convicts (New York: Hyperion, 2002), 184. 63  Extract of a letter from one of the convicts dated 24 July 1790, series 36, Banks Papers, SLNSW.
Women’s lives in a fragmented archive 
113 
The depositions suggest that the crew of the Neptune and the female convicts did form relationships, despite the severe punishments that such arrangements could draw. Churchill described a woman by the name of Jane Haly being beaten ‘mercilessly’; it would appear that a relationship existed between Churchill and Haly, as he mentioned that she was caught with his stockings, which he had given her to mend. However, when Haly was asked to whom they belonged, she did not give Churchill up, which resulted in her receiving a severe beating: 
First Mate Elrington jumped down on her with all his weight as she descended the ladder and beat her mercilessly so as to disfigure her face and make her eyes swell very much. Then Trail beat her and put her in irons around her hands and feet and a collar around her neck fastened with a chain of 14 inches so that she could not stand or lay down for two days. Her crime was that she spoke to one of the crewmembers through a partition.64 
Churchill went on to explain: 
Having given Haley two pairs of stockings to mend, Donald Trail, upon a search found the stockings in her possession and fearing to tell whose stockings they were she said they had been given to her by one of the men whose name she did not know. Trail then threw the stockings overboard.65 
Refractory or outspoken crew or convicts on the Neptune were clearly a beacon for punishment by Trail and Elrington. Throughout the crew’s depositions there seems to be a pattern of abuse when anybody, crew or convicts, spoke up, spoke back, or even if they were slow to comply with an order. The women experienced the same treatment, and two were named in the depositions of the crew members: Jane Elley and Anne Wheeler. Both had been convicted of stealing from shops in London. The nature of their crimes and the way they were described in court indicates that the women were professional sharpers.66 Yet these two stand out from the other convicts beyond their being named in the crew’s depositions. First, they were the only two women of the Second Fleet to have legal counsel at their trial.67 Further, they both spoke during their trials, when not everyone did. Wheeler uttered just thirty-three words; Elley, with 130 words, was the most loquacious of all the Neptune women. Unfortunately for them, such qualities also attracted negative attention on board the Neptune. 
64  There were three other convicts named ‘Jane’ on the Neptune: Jane Edwards, Jane Mulloy and Jane Thompson. I take this reference in the depositions to refer to Jane Elley as ‘Haly’ and ‘Elley’ are close in pronunciation at a time when phonetic spelling regularly produced differences when recording names. TS11/381/1186, 12–18, Churchill deposition. 65  TS11/381/1186, 12–18, Churchill deposition. 66  Taken to mean ‘swindler’. 67  ‘Ann Wheeler’, tried 27 February 1788, Proceedings of the Old Bailey, accessed 23 August 2023, www. oldbaileyonline.org/browse.jsp?id=t17880227-54-defend612&div=t17880227-54#highlight; ‘Jane Elley’, tried 28 October 1789, Proceedings of the Old Bailey, accessed 23 August 2023, www.oldbaileyonline.org/browse.jsp? id=t17891028-72-defend703&div=t17891028-72#highlight.
Australian Journal of Biography and History: No. 8, 2024 
114 
Jane Elley Elley was tried at the Old Bailey on 28 October 1789, accused of grand larceny (stealing, on 23 September, nine yards and three-quarters of sprigged muslin, value ten shillings) and found guilty and sentenced to seven years transportation. Her occupation was recorded as ‘wife’ (her husband was Benjamin Elley) and she had no tattoos.68 It appears that she was pregnant at the time of her arrest, as she spoke of having a belly big with child at her trial: 
I leave it to my counsel; I did not expect to be tried till two o’clock; when I went into this shop, I went with an intent to buy; the gentleman pulled me about sadly, and he put his hands down my bosom, and I thought it was a very indecent action; he asked me where I lived; I told him; and he went and enquired of my character; and he said to me, are you a married woman? I said, yes; says he, it is a pity you have a big belly; I said, it is no matter, I have a good father for it; says he, I wish I was the father of it; and what he has said against me, is as false as God is true.69 
It is not known what happened to the child (whether she gave birth prior to transportation or whether the child died on board), but she did not have a child with her on arrival in Sydney. In the space of two months, Jane had lost the life that she knew and her child. She was literate insofar as she could sign her name, when most women at the time signed their names with a cross.70 
Elley was sent to Norfolk Island six weeks after landing in the colony. Two years later, in 1792, the convict Alexander Dollis, who had arrived in the colony in October 1791, was transferred there; he and Elley became husband and wife and would go on to have four children together. In 1801, Dollis received an absolute pardon and was made superintendent on Norfolk Island. By 1804, the family had returned to Sydney and soon after they disappeared from the archives. It is presumed they returned to England.71

Church register, Parramatta, St John CE. 71  Cathy Dunn, Norfolk Island Baptisms, Burials and Marriages 1801–1806 (Milton: Cathy Dunn, 2014), referencing The National Archives, UK, HO 47/8/83; HRA, series 1, vol. 4, 222; ‘Jane Elley’, Proceedings of the Old Bailey; CSP, SANSW, 9/2731, Fiche 3267, 119; Sydney Gazette, 23 October 1803, 2.
Women’s lives in a fragmented archive 
115 
Churchill, testified that he had frequently seen Trail and William Elrington kick and beat women convicts without mercy; that they beat the women ‘like Man oppos’d to Man in Quarrel’ and, in particular, ‘one Hannah Hawkins in the private parts so  that she did not recover the same when she was landed at Botany Bay nor for weeks afterwards’.72 
Hannah Hawkins (c. 1761–1837) Hannah Hawkins was twenty-eight years old and married when sentenced to death for burglary at Stafford on 13 August 1789. Her sentence was commuted to seven years’ transportation and a few weeks after her trial she was sent aboard the Neptune. She never had children after her arrival in the colony. Perhaps it had something to do with the injury Trail had inflicted upon her. We do not know what she did to attract a beating from the captain, but later glimpses of her in the convict archives paint a picture of a woman not intimidated by authority. Within six weeks of arriving, she was shipped to Norfolk Island, along with two hundred other convicts, where soon after she was sentenced to twenty-five lashes for refusing the overseer’s orders. 
In 1791, she was recorded as cohabiting with a First Fleet convict, Henry Taylor, on a one-acre lot in Sydney Town. By 1801, they had separated. Two years later she emerged from the archives again, having been charged with receiving stolen property. Although she was caught wearing the stolen items, the judge concluded that ‘the evidence was not sufficient to convict her, yet little doubt remained of her being privy to the whole transaction’.73 She was acquitted, but her partner at the time, with whom she was cohabiting, Thomas Thompson, received fourteen years for receiving stolen goods. Hannah is next recorded in 1806 as living with Archibald Kane, sixteen years her junior: he was twenty-eight and she was fortyfour. Kane died in 1823 and Hannah went to live in the poor house, where she died in 1837, aged seventy-five years.74 
Churchill testified that the crew tried to share what they had with the female convicts: 
They frequently sent them a bit of bread and butter. The women, those who could procure an opportunity to do so, were desirous of mending the shirts or stockings of any of the ship’s company [presumably for payment in food].75 
72  TS11/381/1186, 12–18, Churchill deposition. 73  Sydney Gazette and New South Wales Advertiser, 25 September 1803, 2. 74  Cathy Dunn, Romance among the Pine Trees: Marriages November 1791 Norfolk Island (Ulladulla: Australian History Research, 2021); Needham, The Women of the 1790 Neptune. 75  TS11/381/1186, 12–18, Churchill deposition.
Australian Journal of Biography and History: No. 8, 2024 
116 
By the time the ship landed at Cape of Good Hope in April 1790, some four months after leaving England, many of the convicts and the crew were suffering from scurvy (as well as starvation).76 The captain’s cook, Charles King, deposed that when they left Deptford, nine or ten cats had been on board, and that upon asking a convict, Richard Haney, where they were, he was told they had been eaten, ‘boiled without much difficulty in a pudding bag at night’.77 
There is some suggestion that the women were prostituted at Cape of Good Hope, an assertion that Trail denied.78 Beale, in his deposition, spoke of the women ‘being allowed’ to prostitute themselves, as if that was something that they desired—perhaps, given the starving conditions, there were some women who agreed: 
When they landed at the Cape, diverse foreigners were permitted to come and sleep on board and have promiscuous enjoyment with several of the women who were on those occasions permitted to prostitute themselves.79 
Lieutenant John Shapcote, the ineffectual naval agent Shapcote’s job was to ensure the welfare of the convicts, but he proved to be an ineffectual agent in the face of much stronger characters such as Trail. Early in the journey, when the Macarthurs asked Shapcote to intervene on their behalf after a passage from their cabin was boarded up, Shapcote responded that ‘he would not quarrel with Captain Trail for any man’.80 Shapcote’s instructions, as given by the Navy Board, stipulated that he was to be housed on the ship where the women were located, and ‘to see that they are kept separate from the men, and not abused or illtreated, but the contrary’.81 Although ineffectual and beholden to Trail, Shapcote would have been compelled to follow orders and to insist that the women not be distributed among the crew. This is the most likely explanation for Trail going back on his word to the crew, which he knew would create hostility. At a time when one in four ships were at risk of an actual or planned mutiny, this may explain why Trail came down so hard on any crew who so much as spoke to the women.82 
76  August Beyer, surgeon of the Scarborough, lists fifty-seven convicts afflicted with scurvy. See HRNSW, vol. 2, 438. Captain Trail also states that the convicts and crew were suffering both from a violent epidemical fever and scurvy. HRNSW, vol. 2, 804. 77  TS11/381/1186, 49, Charles King deposition. 78  HRNSW, vol. 2, 804, Captain Trail deposition. 79  TS11/381/1186, 1–12, Beale deposition. 80  HRNSW, vol. 2, 491. 81  HRNSW, vol. 2, 437, ‘Instructions to Agent for Transports’. 82  All captains, by necessity, ruled their ships strictly. Of the forty-seven ships that carried convicts to New South Wales between 1787 and 1801 one in four experienced a mutiny, actual or planned, by the convicts and/or soldiers. Gary L. Sturgess, ‘A Government Affair? Reassessing the Management of Australia’s First Fleet. The Second of a Two-Part Analysis’, Great Circle 39, no. 1 (2017): 39.
Women’s lives in a fragmented archive 
117 
Shapcote appeared to pick his battles with Trail. For example, he chose not to intervene in the victualling and treatment of the convicts. This included the shackling of the male convicts. Unlike the First Fleet, where six days out from Britain Captain Arthur Phillip ordered the removal of shackles from male convicts, on the Neptune the male convicts remained shackled for the duration of the journey.83 Further, as Christopher has pointed out, the shackles used in the slave trade were the ones used on the Neptune84 rather than the lighter jail shackles. 
With an overwhelming number of cases of scurvy, both among the crew and the convicts, Shapcote formally wrote to Trail to ask him to stop at the Cape of Good Hope to rest and take on fresh food.85 He had received official requests from all three of the ships’ surgeons to make the stop. On arrival, the Second Fleet discovered that the storeship, Guardian, bound for Botany Bay with much needed food stores, had hit an iceberg and had only narrowly made it to the Cape. The commander of the Guardian, Lieutenant Riou, negotiated with Shapcote to send the Guardian’s stores aboard the Neptune but met with resistance. He later remarked: 
if ever the navy make another contract like that of the last three ships they ought be shot and as for their agent Mr Shapcote he behaved here just as foolishly as a man could well do.86 
At the Cape, Shapcote wrote that the deaths numbered one woman and forty-five men aboard the Neptune. A further fifteen and eight male convicts from the Scarborough and Surprize, respectively, had died by the time the ships reached the Cape.87 However, during the eight-week voyage from the Cape to Sydney, deaths on board those ships would increase exponentially. On the Neptune, a further one hundred men and fourteen women died. Trail’s deposition references a disease raging among the convicts and the ship’s company as the cause. It is evident that scurvy was overtaking the convicts, and Trail’s decision to push on rather than rest and recuperate the convicts on fresh provisions was the likely cause of so many deaths. Shapcote never made it to Sydney Cove. He also died during that passage, but Trail refuted suggestions that he had met ‘with an untimely death’.88 The journey from the Cape to Sydney was cold, and the ship was buffeted by storms. There were accounts of many male convicts being up to their waists in water. Some were said to have drowned 
83  Sturgess, ‘A Government Affair’, 33. 84  Christopher, ‘The Slave Trade’. 85  Letters from surgeons, William Gray Neptune, William Waters Surprize, John Harris of the New South Wales Corps, A. J. Beyer of the Scarborough, in Accounts and Papers, 324–6. 86  Michael Flynn, ‘Second Fleet’, Dictionary of Sydney, 2016, accessed 17 October 2022, dictionaryofsydney.org/ entry/second_fleet. 87  HRNSW, vol. 1, part 2 (1783–1792), 334. 88  HRNSW, vol. 2, 804, Captain Trail deposition. Shapcote did not survive and nor did his journal. He was instructed by the Navy Board: 
You are to take care that every circumstance worth reciting is inserted in your journals during your employment on this service, noting the death of such as may happen to die on the passage. 
An extract of a warrant from the Navy Board to Lieutenant Shapcote, 17 November 1789, in Accounts and Papers, 322.
Australian Journal of Biography and History: No. 8, 2024 
118 
while shackled below on the orlop deck, possibly too weak to rouse themselves when the ship began to take on water. When the ship finally limped into port, Phillip’s description of the Neptune as the Wilful Murder was indeed an apt one. 
Anne Wheeler was the only other woman mentioned in the depositions. Evidently a woman of some means, at the time of her arrest for shoplifting she was described as wearing ‘a very large white silk cloak … trimmed with furr, and a very large muff’.89 When boarding the Neptune, she brought with her a trunk. Charles King testified: 
It was a very good trunk filled with many valuable articles for housekeeping such as sheets, tablecloths. Trail thought fit to say the poor woman had stolen them from him and took several of them from her.90 
According to Trail, he had confiscated Wheeler’s trunk a few weeks out from Sydney because he believed she had stolen a tablecloth from him. When they arrived, he ordered the trunk to be brought out from the gun room and told his steward to let Mrs Wheeler have all her things again, even the piece of tablecloth in dispute. However, when opened her trunk was found to swarm with bugs, so he threw it overboard.91 This trunk was the only one thrown overboard belonging to the female convicts. 
Ann Wheeler and Elizabeth Barnsley Anne Wheeler was tried with Elizabeth Barnsley in February 1788 for shoplifting from a Bond Street drapery and sentenced to seven years’ transportation. Both she and Barnsely could afford the weekly fee charged to stay on the Master’s side of Newgate Prison. Wheeler and Barnsley were due to be transported on the Lady Juliana, but the former fell ill shortly before embarkation and was kept behind. John Nicol, a mariner on the Lady Juliana, described Barnsley as a well-known sharper and a queen among the convicts.92 Wheeler and Barnsley were close friends, quite literally ‘thick as thieves’. They lived together at Newgate in relative comfort for eighteen months before they were separated and transported on different ships. Wheeler was sent to Norfolk Island shortly after arriving in the colony. The following year, in 1791, Barnsley and her husband, Thomas Barnsley, and child, followed her there. In 1794, all of them left Norfolk Island together— Wheeler and the Barnsely family, thereafter disappearing from the historical record, presumably because they returned to England. If so, Wheeler was one of just five women from the Neptune believed to have returned to England. 
89  ‘Ann Wheeler, Elizabeth Barnsley’, Proceedings of the Old Bailey, accessed 11 August 2023, www.oldbaileyonline. org/browse.jsp?id=t17880227-54-defend614&div=t17880227-54#highlight. 90  TS11/381/1186, 49, Charles King deposition. 91  HRNSW, vol. 2; Accounts and Papers, 310–14. 92  Rees, The Floating Brothel.
Women’s lives in a fragmented archive 
119 
There is some evidence to suggest that the condition of the convicts prior to boarding was a key factor in their survival. For instance, none of the four women previously interned at Chester Castle Gaol survived the journey. Did the conditions at the gaol lead to their demise as much as the journey? According to John Howard, who inspected prisons in England, the cells at Chester Castle into which prisoners were locked at night were seven-and-a-half-feet deep by three-and-a-half-feet wide (two point three metres x one metre—not much larger than a king single bed by today’s standards). Into this, three to four prisoners were confined. There were no windows and, according to Howard, ‘not a breath of fresh air’. Located effectively in the cellar, the cells were accessed by descending twenty-one steps from the small prison yard. There was no infirmary, no surgeon and no straw to lie on.93 Kippen and Maxwell-Stewart argue that the distance travelled from the prisons to the transports also played a part in health prior to embarkation; they showed that the distances convicts were required to travel, usually in an open wagon, and, in this case, in winter, had a material impact on their survivability.94 In total, fifteen convicts from Chester Castle Gaol boarded the Second Fleet: eleven men and four women. Only two men survived the journey to Australia; one died after a year in the colony and the other, John Hughes, died in 1828 at Windsor, New South Wales, aged sixty-five.95 
It cannot be mere coincidence that thirteen of fifteen convicts from Chester Castle died on the journey to Australia. Trail’s cruelty is indisputable, but this small subset indicates that existing health and wellness also played a role. The introduction of better controls—specifically, the appointment of navy-approved surgeon superintendents to travel with convicts and tighter stipulations about their treatment enroute—saw a marked improvement in convict mortality.96 Shapcote failed in his duty to care for the convicts during transportation and his unwillingness or inability to stand up to Trail and the other senior members of the ship’s company was a major factor in this. 
A ‘perfect sea monster’ Elizabeth Macarthur described Trail as a ‘perfect sea monster’.97 The male convicts suffered under the conditions imposed by Trail far more than could reasonably be expected, even taking account of Trail’s assertions that there was the threat of surprise 
93  The State of the Prisons in England and Wales: With Preliminary Observations, and an Account of Some Foreign Prisons (Warrington: John Howard, printed by William Eyres, 1777), 443. The women from Chester Castle Gaol who died en route were Mary Bather, Betty Bradley, Rebecca Heathcote and Hannah Prince. 94  Hamish Maxwell-Stewart and Rebecca Kippen, ‘Sickness and Death on Male and Female Convict Voyages to Australia’, accessed 30 June 2023, www.femaleconvicts.org.au/docs/seminars/Voyages_HamishMaxwellStewart.pdf. 95  Australian Convict Transportation Registers, Second Fleet, 1789-90 H.O 11/1. 96  Navy appointed surgeons were introduced in 1815. For further reading on this see McDonald and Shlomowitz, ‘Mortality on Convict Voyages’; Mark Staniforth, ‘Care and Control: Female Convict Transportation Voyages to Van Diemen’s Land, 1818–1853’, Great Circle 16 (1994): 23–42; Maxwell-Stewart and Kippen, ‘Sickness and Death’, in Lives in Transition. 97  Elizabeth Macarthur to her mother, 20 April 1790, in Onslow, Some Early Records, 17.
Australian Journal of Biography and History: No. 8, 2024 
120 
(i.e. mutiny).98 The slave shackles worn for the duration of the journey; the lack of exercise; the conditions below ship, of both suffocating heat at the equator and freezing conditions in the Southern Ocean; the withholding of rations; the lack of sanitation; and the beatings all coalesced to make the convicts highly vulnerable to disease and death. Captain Hill, a marine, reflected: 
The slave trade is merciful compared with what I have seen on this fleet … the more they can withhold from the unhappy Wretches, the more Provisions they have to dispose of in a foreign market; and the earlier in the voyage they die, the longer they can draw the deceased’s allowance.99 
The crew, too, experienced beatings and scanty rations. Indeed, Trail was brought to trial for the murder of two of his seamen. The women were also deprived of food and water and experienced beatings the same way Trail and Elrington would beat a man. Some of the crew expressed gentler feelings towards the women and a desire to share food from their own meagre allowances and a willingness to talk with and engage on a  social level, even after it became clear that Trail would brook no fraternising between crew and female convicts. Twenty-five per cent of Neptune’s women died on the journey. Notwithstanding the multitude of factors that can impact survivability on such a difficult journey, there is no doubt that Trail’s particular handling and attitudes towards the convicts was a major factor in the mortality rates on the Neptune. 
Trail did not face a magistrate. Instead, he supplied his own written deposition. The prosecutor, Thomas Evans, accused him of remaining incognito or abroad until all the witnesses were gone to sea.100 Both Trail and Elrington were ultimately acquitted. Evans was ordered to be struck off for his handling of the prosecution.101 Trail rejoined the Royal Navy, where he subsequently fought in the French Revolutionary Wars (1792–1802). He would later return to slave trading while working at Cape Town.102 There are many factors that could contribute to the mortality of convicts on board ship, but I argue that chief among them was the captain’s management of the ship and the cargo, and the shipboard rules he employed.
